===== Module 1: Understanding Medications using the Gastrointestinal System [_7758061_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758061_1?view=content&state=view
Module 1 lays down the foundation for your understanding of medications, your ability to use them safely and your ability to streamline your studies in Pharmacology. Using one of the easiest bodily systems, the gastrointestinal tract/system (GIT), we explore pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics and other foundational information. This is a very important module that is twice the size of the average module in this unit. The week 2 quiz on Friday of week 2 tests your knowledge of the module.
Children:
Module overview
Chapter 1.1: Medications that work by chemical or direct action
Chapter 1.2: Intro to Pharmacodynamics: Medications that work on cellular receptors
Chapter 1.3: Intro to Pharmacokinetics: Absorption, distribution, metabolism and excretion of drugs
Chapter 1.4: Medications that exert their effects on enzymes
Chapter 1.5 CONDENSED (ONLY the ASSESSABLE material from: Medications that affect neurotransmitters
Chapter 1.5: Medications that affect neurotransmitters (FULL Chapter including non-compulsory lessons)

===== Module overview [_7758152_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758088_1?view=content&state=view
Module 1: The basics of Pharmacology
Hi All,
Module 1 explores most of the important basics of Pharmacology to give you a solid foundation for your understanding of medications in the future. Normally, the foundational information in pharmacology is the most difficult (and sometimes boring) material.
But in this module, we use the medications of the gastrointestinal system/tract (GIT) to explore these difficult topics, making it actually fun. We start out with the easiest of medications - those that cannot get absorbed and work by chemical action or by direct action. (Normally, our medications work on cellular receptors or affect enzymes, but the first topic/chapter introduces you very slowly.) Next, Topic/Chapter 2, we explore cellular receptors, looking at what they are, what they do and how our drugs can affect them. This is where we get into the topic of pharmacodynamics. The third topic/chapter starts introducing you to pharmacokinetics, the movement of drugs through the system, involving absorption, distribution, metabolism and excretion. We look at enzymes and how we can affect them with our medications in Topic/chapter 4. Finally, in Topic/chapter 5, we look at neurotransmitters and the most difficult concepts in the GIT, which have to do with our antiemetics.
If you want to love pharmacology the way I do, please pay close attention to this first module. I teamed up with a partner in this module to make sure that you can actually picture the processes that will form the basis of your understanding of pharmacology. Unfortunately, while all of the videos in this unit are of relatively high quality, this module will have a significantly higher level of annimation than subsequent modules, but by then you will have developed the ability of picturing how our medications work !
Enjoy! Regards,
Ken
Module purpose
By the end of this module you will be able to:
Explain the main principles of pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics
Describe the mechanism of action, pharmacological effects, therapuetic uses, contraindications, safety issues and side effects of the most important medications of the GIT
A note about the size of this module
This entire module contains a total of 4 hours of video lessons plus activities that reinforce your knowledge and prepare you for the quiz. The average of all 6 modules is 2 hours of video lessons per module, so this is significantly bigger than any other module. While the online material is much bigger this week than other weeks, we do not have any tutorial questions or questions in the Case Study this week, so your work this week is in the modules.

https://learn.scu.edu.au/bbcswebdav/pid-7758152-dt-content-rid-88840952_2/xid-88840952_2

===== Introduction to Module 1: Chapter 1 [_7758357_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758153_1?view=content&state=view
Introduction
Normally, medications work on cellular receptors or by affecting enzymes. They go through the process of pharmacokinetics, which involves absorption, distribution, metabolism and excretion. So, understanding them is pretty difficult. In this topic/chapter, we are going to start out with the easiest medications of the digestive system, the antacids and the laxatives. Since these do not get absorbed and they work by chemical or direct action rather on cellular receptors or enzymes, they are in themselves, relatively easy to understand. But remember that as you go through these topics, you are learning the foundation for your understanding of all medications, so please follow the step-by-step process of going through all of the material presented here.
In this module, the only NOTES that are available are notes: three to a page with spaces for your own notes. In subsequent modules, PowerPoints and notes will be available.
Click directly below to access the notes, which are arranged in order of the way the material is presented, from the introductory video lesson to the summary lesson.
The importance of engagement
The obvious reason to engage in the videos and activities in this module is that
they are the way that you will most efficiently prepare for the 20% quiz
, but the videos and activities will also help in several other ways.
Video: Why engage in the quizzes after each video?
Watch this video to see how the process works to help your memory of the facts that are most important for your future career.

[79.68] You really remember to tell the person to take the medication with plenty of water.
[85.04] Understanding has just made remembering a lot easier.
[90.36] The next level up from understanding is applying.
[93.68] I may ask you to predict what would happen if the person taking this medication was on a diuretic, or sometimes called a water pill.
[104.80] Do you see how, in order to answer that question, you need to go through that scenario in your mind?
[110.72] Well, the person on a diuretic or water pill is losing a lot of fluid through their increased urination.
[117.40] So they may be a little dehydrated. And if they take the medication while they're on the diuretic, they may wind up with kidney damage.
[127.64] That is the thought process that you would go through in order to answer the question.
[133.28] And as you go through that thought process, you reinforce your understanding of the material and you reinforce your memory of the important points.

===== Activity: Overview of antacids [_7758358_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758154_1?view=content&state=view
What are antacids?
Right now, we are learning how to study pharmacology in the most productive way. If we develop the best approach to studying medicines using the easiest medications, we can apply that to other more difficult medications and disorders.
Antacids are indicated foar the occasional relief of heartburn and contraindicated as the heartburn (or gastroesophageal reflux) continues for an extended length of time (becomes chronic). In this lesson, we will understand why that is, because what we don't want to do is memorise long lists of what medications are indicated for and contraindicated in.
So please don't think that the topic is not valuable just because it is relatively easy!
Chapter overview
This first chapter is pretty easy, so you may want to speed things up. In this module, I put most of the videos on YouTube, so turning up or down the speed is just a bit different to the way in which you will turn up the speed in other modules.
To adjust the speed of this video, wait until the video starts, and then press the settings tab (the cog wheel) and choose PLAYBACK SPEED, which gives you options from 0.25 (1/4th of the original speed) to 2.0 (twice the original speed). Simply choose the one that is most appropriate for you. Just as some people read faster or slower than others, no vidoe speed will be perfect for everyone, so use this feature to get the most for the least amount of time. After finishing the video, make sure that you engage in the activity below to reinforce that knowledge and get ready for the 30% quiz. I only use information found in the videos and activities when I design the quiz!
Video: Overview of antacids
Activity: Test your knowledge

The rebound hyperacidity of the antacids can contribute to the development of chronic problems like dysplastic changes in the oesophagus (called Barrett's disease or Barrett's Oesophagus)

The rebound hyperacidity may contribute to the development of a peptic ulcer

The client may not get proper medical advice, resulting in the advancement of dysplastic changes in the oesophagus

Antacids taken incorrectly can affect the absorption of some nutrients, resulting in nutritional deficiencies.

===== Specific antacids [_7758156_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758156_1?view=content&state=view

===== Activity: Specific antacids [_7758360_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758156_1?view=content&state=view
Types of antacids
There are four types of antacids that are frequently used in Australia. But how do you know which is best for the individual? For instance, if client A is normally a bit constipated, they would do best by taking the magnesium antacids, and if client B generally has softer stools, they may do better with the aluminium antacids. This lesson gives you a clear picture of the strengths and weaknesses of all four antacids so that you can decide which, if any, is best for the individual client.
Video: Specific antacids
Please watch this 5 minute video (or 2.5 minutes turned up to 2x speed) and then engage in the activities below.
Activity: Test your knowledge

Which of the following antacids is/are ABSORBED, potentially resulting in greater side effects?

Untitled Multiple Choice

Magnesium hydroxide is an antacid that is osmotically active and not absorbed. As such it causes diarrhoea in many people.

Aluminium hydroxide or citrate is an antacid that causes constipation and also binds to many drugs and nutrients so care should be taken not to take them with meals or medications.

Calcium carbonate has the strongest antacid effects but also has the strongest rebound hyperacidity

Sodium bicarbonate is the preferred antacid because it is not absorbed

Select the answer or answers that is/are correct:

Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758157_1?view=content&state=view
Introduction to the laxatives
We continue with our exploration into the easiest of our medications with a look at the laxatives.
Laxatives are the number 1 most abused medications because they are OTC (over the counter) and clients often self-prescribe when they should just be consuming more water and eating more fibre. Interestingly, there are a number of safety issues about the use of laxatives, so in this portion of the topic/chapter, we will learn a lot about how to safely suggest a medication with relatively easy examples. Remember, we are not just learning about laxatives, as we will be applying the information that we learn to other medications, conditions and scenarios.
Video: Introduction to laxatives
This lesson begins the second half of the chapter, and as such I present the aims and general approach. Please watch the short 2.5-minute video and follow it up by quizzing yourself below. Remember that I made notes, 3 to a page to help you (all the way at the top of module), which you may find useful to jot down important points as you go through the videos. Writing things down has a really strong ability to reinforce knowledge.
Activity: Test your knowledge

Which of the following is the FIRST LINE treatment for constipation?

Untitled Multiple Choice

true

The term first line treatment is the one accepted as the best treatment. If it doesn’t cure the disease or give significant relief, or it causes severe side effects for the individual client, other treatment may be added or used instead. Other terms can be used including induction therapy, primary therapy, and primary treatment.

Understanding constipation
Remember that we need to:
know the general physiology of the area and
aim to understand a bit about the disorder (the PATHO-physiology) that the medication we are studying is used for.
Before we can understand why our medications are being used, how they will work and in what circumstances they will be contraindicated. The alternative is to memorise long lists of drugs that we don't understand at all. While we are talking about constipation in this instance, which is relatively easy, we will apply these techniques to be able to understand much more difficult disorders and drugs.
This section allows you to understand constipation so that you can safely recommend the best therapy.

Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758159_1?view=content&state=view
Osmotic laxatives
Can you really picture the process of osmosis? If so, that is great, you will understand the osmotic laxatives quite easily. If not, this video will help you picture the process as you learn about the osmotic laxatives with this lesson. One of the most important points about osmotic laxatives is the fact that they develop a linear dose response. You will find out what this is and why it is very uncommon with our medications and very dangerous to assume that our other medications have that type of response.
In the quiz at the end of the Term, I will definitely include a question about linear dose response and other material presented in this 6-minute video. Please watch it to learn more about osmosis, safety issues of osmotic laxatives, linear dose response curve, normal dose response of medications and even the safety issues of paracetamol. I cover so much important information in this first module, so please give each of lessons your best shot.

--- h5p_c4850f70737a9dc3cb67 ---
Osmotic laxatives include lactulose and magnesium citrate

The osmotic laxatives exhibit a LINEAR dose-response curve, meaning the more you take, the greater the effect.

The osmotic laxatives result in more fluid in the GIT, and that fluid comes from the blood (plasma)

Since the osmotic laxatives do not get absorbed and they simply work by direct action, there are no precautions needed when taking them.

Finish ?

Retry ?

Choose the statement or statements about osmotic laxatives that is/are correct:

Untitled Multiple Choice

The standard dose curve often 'peaks' shortly after the therapeutic dose.

Paracetamol is a drug that exhibits a standard dose curve. The therapeutic dose is very close to the maximum effect that can be achieved with that mild analgesic. This means that if the client takes more than the standard dose, they may only experience the toxic effects rather than getting additional relief from pain.

The osmotic diuretics demonstrate a dose response curve, meaning the more you take, the greater the effect. There are few examples of that type of response. Select the correct statement(s) about the standard dose curve:

===== Stimulant laxatives [_7758160_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758160_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Activity: Stimulant laxatives

===== Activity: Stimulant laxatives [_7758364_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758160_1?view=content&state=view
Stimulant laxatives
If you have ever had a GIT 'bug' and ended up going to the toilet about 20 times, you may have wondered why that was happening. Well, the toxins from the bacteria and the bacteria themselves are basically working on the enteric nervous system of the GIT to signal an increased motility and increased secretions. We want to get those toxins out ASAP!
That is a simplistic picture of what the stimulant laxatives do. They irritate the GIT to really get the intestines moving.
But think about that. It is really logical when you think about them, that chronic (long term) use of the stimulant laxatives would result in damage to the GIT. Find out more about the stimulant laxatives and the very short list of situations in which they are indicated by participating in this lesson.
But wait, there is more! (there is always more in this module, so try not to skip anything). We talk about enteric coating, (why some drugs use an enteric coating, how to take drugs that are enteric coated, and what not to do with them) as well.
In the above description of this lesson, I note a number of important points that you will learn by watching this 7 minute video and engaging in the activities.
Video: Stimulant Laxatives: Sodium Picosulfate, Senna, Castor oil and Bisacodyl
Activity: Test your knowledge

Choose the stimulant laxative(s) that is/are effective if taken orally, by suppository, or by enema.

Untitled Multiple Choice

They should only be used when nothing else works or as a bowel preparation prior to surgery or endoscopy

They often result in a strong rebound constipation

When used over a long time, the smooth muscles adapt to the level of stimulation

They can result in electrolyte abnormalities

If used over a long period of time (several years) normal bowel habits can be re-established by increasing fluid and fibre intake

Choose the correct statement(s) about the stimulant laxatives:

true

Enteric coatings are placed on medications that need to be dissolved in the intestines rather than the stomach. One should never chew a medication with an enteric coating.

Retry ?

The mineral oil laxatives greatly reduce the absorption of the fat soluble vitamins. Which of the following is/are fat soluble vitamin(s)?

Untitled Multiple Choice

true

The main safety concern about mineral oil and detergent laxatives like docusate is that they should never be used together, as that allows some of the mineral oil to be absorbed. Once in the body, there is no method of excretion, and the immune system will form granulomas in an attempt to 'hide' the mineral oil from the rest of the body.

===== Introduction to Chapter 2 [_7758367_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758163_1?view=content&state=view
Introduction to Pharmacodynamics
Now that we understand some of the foundational information about medications, we can move on to the more complicated topics of Pharmacology, namely Pharmacodynamics. Pharmacodynamics basically means 'what the drug does to the body' - how does it work?
Most of our medications work on cellular receptors, so it is important that in this chapter, we get a good, clear picture of what the cellular receptors are and how our medications can use those to exert their pharmacological effects.
The NOTES for this portion of the Module are directly below.
Video: Introduction to chapter 2
Watch this introduction to Chapter 2 of Module 1, and reinforce your knowledge with the activity.
Activity: Test your knowledge

===== Drug nomenclature (Naming) [_7758165_1] =====

Children:
Drug nomenclature (Naming)

===== Drug nomenclature (Naming) [_7758369_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758165_1?view=content&state=view
Naming conventions
There are many different names for the same medication, so do we need to learn them all? Are there any tricks that we can learn to help us remember the most important drugs?
While we will no doubt need to know the common brand names of many of our medications, it is the generic name that is most important in the first instance, because that is the one that is always on the label. There are affixes (usually suffixes) to start looking out for and this video attempts to get you in the habit of finding that suffix, and identifying the drug.
Video: Drug nomenclature (naming)
After you watch this video and do the associated activities, you will never look at a drug name the same way that you do now!

The generic name of a drug is the shortened version of the chemical name. It often has an affix (usually a suffix that comes at the end of the name) that can identify the drug class that the medication belongs to.

Comparing Loperamide to other opioids

===== Comparing Loperamide to other opioids [_7758370_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758166_1?view=content&state=view
Understanding Loperamide
We have all heard about the opioids. The drug class that includes some of the strongest painkillers that we have. But Loperamide is an opioid but in normal doses, it has not pain-relieving properties. So why is that?
We compare Loperamide to other opioids in this lesson so that you can get an overall picture of the drug class called the opioids.
We also investigate an important safety issue about loperamide: remember what we said, that 1/3rd of 1% of the medication gets into the system? Some people try to get an opioid high from consuming several times the recommended dose. Find out what happens in this lesson.
Video: Comparing Loperamide to other opioids
Despite the fact that an average of 0.4% of loperamide gets absorbed, there are important safety issues that you will learn about as you engage in this video. Remember that you can speed up or slow down the videos by clicking on the settings tab, and choosing a setting different to 1.0.

--- h5p_9fbef4a0df9187571e38 ---
When the cellular receptor is bound by the natural ligand, or a drug that mimics the natural ligand, that is the signal for the cell to do something.

The cellular receptors that most of our drugs bind to in order to perform their therapeutic action are involved in communication between other cells or other parts of the body

Cellular receptors can be increased or decreased by the cell, thereby increasing or decreasing the number of messages that to to the cell (provided there are adequate ligands to bind to those receptors).

Although there are slight variations in cellular receptors, they are all quite similar.

Finish ?

Retry ?

Choose the following that is/are correct:

Untitled Multiple Choice

false

While the cell has the ability to increase or decrease the number of receptors on the surface, it never does this in response to drugs.

Untitled True/False Question

true

In Pharmacology, when we are discussing cellular receptors, the ligand is the natural substance for which the substance is made, and it can be a small molecule, ion, or protein.

===== Affinity potency selectivity [_7758168_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758168_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Affinity potency selectivity

===== Affinity potency selectivity [_7758372_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758168_1?view=content&state=view
Understanding affinity, potency and selectivity
Why is it that medication A, which works in the same way as Medication B can be so much stronger (more potent)? While there can be many reasons, one of the most common has to do with the strength of the molecular bond of the medication to its given receptor.
Most often, a medication that affects cellular receptors binds to its receptor and then lets go shortly after. Then it binds to another receptor of the same type, and lets go... But what if the drug bound to the receptor and it really takes a lot more time to get it off that receptor (it had a much greater affinity to the receptor)? As you can imagine, many more messages will be sent to the cell. So the drug's affinity can affect its potency.
This is another really important lesson as we learn three very important concepts, affinity, potency and selectivity.
Video: Affinity potency selectivity
The terms you learn by watching this video will be used throughout this unit. Make sure that you engage in the activities directly after watching the video.
(Please also note the two questions within the video associated with this lesson)
Activity: Test your knowledge

The binding of our drugs to cellular receptors depends on the general shape of the molecule as well as the attractant and repelling molecular forces. Which of the following is the strongest bond in this list?

Untitled Multiple Choice

Affinity

Potency

Selectivity

Which of the following is a measure of the strength of the bonding between a receptor and its ligand (or a drug)?

true

A drug with a higher affinity to the receptor is likely to stay on the receptor for longer, which often results in an increase in the potency, or strength of the medication.

Untitled True/False Question

We are always looking for more selective medications, because they only bind to the specific receptor that they are made for, giving fewer side effects.

--- h5p_4b943ed45b37cee5c540 ---
It can act as an agonist, acting like the natural ligand, and performing the same physiological response as the natural ligand.

It can act as an antagonist, binding to the receptor and just blocking it, competing with the natural ligand and thereby decreasing the messages that are sent to the cell.

On certain receptors, it can act as an inverse agonist, which is simplistically referred to by many authors as an antagonist.

It can act as an inhibitor

--- h5p_31cefe408564c5abf557 ---
Loperamide (Imodium) is an opioid receptor antagonist because it decreases the transmission of impulses from the GIT

While most opioids can decrease diarrhoea, Loperamide is the preferred medication for diarrhoea (after of course, fluid and electrolytes have been administered if necessary) because very little of it gets into systemic circulation.

Loperamide activates opioid receptors on the enteric nervous system, resulting in an inhibition of gastric emptying, increase in sphincter tone, and blockade of peristalsis. As such, it decreases diarrhoea in those with diarrhoea, or causes constipation in those who have normal bowel habits at the time.

--- h5p_9de6cfb9ec1b1a3474cd ---
Different modes of administration are absorbed at different rates, and that is important because it has implications on the number of side effects that will be likely and the speed at which the drug is metabolised. This excercise makes sure that you understand the approximate speed with which each method of administration is absorbed.

Untitled Drag the Words

Cmax or concentration max is the maximum (or peak) serum concentration that a drug achieves

A mode of administration that achieves a very high c-max or concentration max is going to result in the drug being metabolised faster in the initial stage of metabolism.

There is a greater potential for overdose, side effects and complications with the mode of administration that gets the medication into the system the fastest.

When a drug is in high concentration, it is less likely to bind to receptors or enzymes that it is not supposed to bind to.

Even if the properties of the drug favour absorption in the stomach, as they do with aspirin, very little of that medication is actually absorbed in the stomach.

Hydrophilic (water soluble) drugs are the type of drugs that are absorbed the best from the GIT

When a drug is taken with food, it normally decreases the bioavailability (the fraction of the drug that reaches the systemic circulation unchanged) of the drug

An ointment is an oil based topical preparation. If it includes a medication that must be absorbed, that medication needs to be a lipophilic drug

The drug would go through the liver to be metabolised, which could be problematic, because often times the drug is provided as a suppository to AVOID the liver.

Untitled Multiple Choice

Lipophilic (oil based) drugs often access a protein carrier molecule so that they can be carried through the circulation.

Each drug is different as to the % of the drug is bound to a carrier protein. Sometimes it is a high protein binding (for instance, 99%) and other times it is a low protein binding (for instance, 10%)

Only the unbound portion of the drug (the drug that is not bound to the proteins) is active and it is only the unbound portion of the drug that can be metabolised.

The % of the drug that is bound to proteins will always remain bound to the proteins until it is degraded in the body

Please choose the statement(s) that is/are correct.

===== Metabolism of drugs [_7758175_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758175_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Metabolism of drugs

===== Metabolism of drugs [_7758379_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758175_1?view=content&state=view
The importance of the liver
The liver is the site of most metabolism of drugs. As soon as most drugs are absorbed and begin the distribution process, our body wants to excrete the drug as quickly as possible. And since the kidneys are the main site of excretion of most drugs, the main goal of metabolism of drugs is to make the drug more water soluble and easy to excrete. We want that drug out of there ASAP
Interestingly, as drugs go through the liver, many different scenarios are possible, including that an inactive pro-drug can become an active drug or an active drug can become an inactive drug. This lesson explains the basics of the third principle of Pharmacokinetics, the metabolism of drugs, in a way that you can apply the information to any medication.
Video: Metabolism of drugs
Please watch this video and engage in the three activities in the following sections.
Activity: Test your knowledge

===== Excretion of drugs [_7758176_1] =====

===== Excretion of drugs [_7758380_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758176_1?view=content&state=view
How drugs are excreted
The main thing that we need to learn about with respect to the excretion of drugs has to do with the half-life. Half-life is defined as the time required for any specified property (e.g. the concentration of a substance in the body) to decrease by half. Interestingly almost all of our prescription medications are eliminated differently to that of ethanol, the alcohol in alcoholic beverages.
Video: Excretion of drugs
I have condensed the excretion of drugs so what is left (5 minutes) is really important. Please engage in this lesson.

Choose the correct answer for a drug that has a half-life of 4 hours.
If there is 4 mg of medication in the system at noon, when will there be an equivalent of 1 mg in the system?

Untitled Multiple Choice

true

Most drugs are metabolised using First order kinetics, meaning that in the first half life, half of the drug is excreted. Then, the timer starts again, and half of the remaining dose is excreted in the second half life. After 4.5 half lifes, about 5% of the drug remains.

Untitled True/False Question

3 hours

7 or more hours, depending on the person

Ethanol (the alcohol that is in wine and beer) is metabolised by zero order kinetics meaning that it is initially metabolised in a linear way, with about one standard drink metabolised per hour. But after it has been metabolised to the point that there is only about < 1 standard drink, it is metabolised by first order kinetics.
Given that, how long would it take a P plater to metabolise 3 standard drinks to the point that they have 0 blood alcohol?

===== Understanding the side effects of reducing stomach acid [_7758178_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758178_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Understanding the side effects of reducing stomach acid

===== Understanding the side effects of reducing stomach acid [_7758382_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758178_1?view=content&state=view
The importance of stomach acid
Histamine 2 blockers (the drug class with the ...tidine suffix) do not block all of the acid from the stomach like proton pump inhibitors (the ...prazole drugs) do, but nonetheless, they have similar long-term side effects associated with the greatly reduced stomach acid.
If you think about it, the acid is there for a reason. And some clients will be blocking that acid production for years. So let's quickly review the functions of the acid in the stomach and see how that ties into the side effects that are likely from long-term decreases in stomach acid.
Video: Understanding side effects of reduced stomach acid
Millions of Australians use these medications that are available without a prescription and without knowing the potential side effects associated with their long term use. Understand those effects in just 5 minutes with this video, and you will be able to help clients or friends who decrease their GIT acidity without getting the problem properly assessed.

===== Histamine 2 blockers [_7758179_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758179_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Histamine 2 blockers

===== Histamine 2 blockers [_7758383_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758179_1?view=content&state=view
Safety issue
The most popular Histamine 2 receptor blockers (due to better efficacy and potency) was Ranitidine (Zantac) until 2020 when it was discovered that some components of the drug broke down into carcinogenic components. This happened to a greater extent over time and if the drug was stored improperly, like in a hot car. The company that manufactured Zantac has changed the drug from Ranitidine to Famotidine and changed the brand name of their version of Famotidine to Zantac 360. Clients who mention having Zantac should be questioned to make sure they are not taking leftover Ranitidine.
Video: Histamine 2  receptor blockers
All other Histamine 2 blockers like Famotidine and Cimetidine and other ...tidine drugs have been tested for carcinogens and none were found. This video excludes mention of Ranitidine and attempts to give you a good picture of the other members of the Drug Class called the Histamine 2 receptor blocker drugs.
Activity: Test your knowledge

--- h5p_d0e4e2cb805b0a8125d3 ---
Histamine 2 receptor antagonists work on a specific cell called the parietal cell that is located in gastric pits of the lining of the stomach.

Histamine 2 receptor antagonists affect the parietal cell directly, without having to be absorbed, because the medication is exposed to those cells when it is in the stomach.

The parietal cells have a trans-membrane protein carrier called a hydrogen potassium ATPase pump that secrete the H+ (sometimes called protons; sometimes called ‘acid’) into the gastric pits and on to the stomach, but need three things to bind to the cell at the same time in order for that to occur, Histamine 2, Acetylcholine (from the parasympathetic rest and digest system) and gastrin (Gastrin is a hormone that is released when there are peptides in the stomach.).

The Histamine 2 receptor blockers like Famotidine and other ….tidine drugs are competitive antagonists, so they compete with the Histamine 2 that is in the area of the parietal cell. There is still some Histamine 2 binding in the parietal cell and still some acid being secreted, so it will never be 100% effective,

--- h5p_21de6f1d50d4790e553a ---
While most of our enzymatic inhibitors are reversible, there are irreversible inhibitors as well

If an irreversible inhibitor is used, the drug needs to be degraded at the active site or the enzyme needs to be replaced by the cell, meaning that the effect of the drug will last a lot longer than the half life of the drug would suggest.

Enzymes are proteins that catalyse or speed up a biological process

Irreversible enzymatic inhibitors last for the life of the cell involved.

Finish ?

Retry ?

Choose the correct statement(s) about enzymes and our medications that inhibit them

A review of Pharmacokinetics
Before we get on to the discussion of enzymes and enzymatic inhibitors that are the main topic of this chapter, let's take a review of the last chapter's topic, but using the drug we will be discussing in this chapter. The material in this video is a review and as such is not mandatory, but make sure that you engage in the activities, as those are assessable.
So in this lesson, we use Omeprazole, (a proton pump inhibitor that decreases stomach acid) to review all of the processes involved in Pharmacokinetics: absorption, distribution, metabolism and excretion. You don’t need to memorise anything, just sit back and watch the drug flow through the system as you review the processes involved in one of the most difficult topics in Pharmacology.
Video: Pharmacokinetics - Visualising Omerprazole
This is an interesting review of the four processes of pharmacokinetics, using omeprazole as the drug. Sit back and watch it get absorbed, distributed, metabolised and excreted. This video follows a drug (omeprazole) through the body as a review of pharmacokinetics, and as such it is not compulsory but make sure that you engage in the activities directly below, which cover a couple points that were not discussed elsewhere.

--- h5p_75af652ef200509562a7 ---
Enteric coatings are placed on the medications mostly if they are irritating to the stomach or they need to dissolve in the small intestine so that they are protected from the stomach acids.

Enteric coated medications must never be chewed, broken in half, or crushed.

Enteric coatings are placed on the medication to make their effects last longer.

Finish ?

Retry ?

Which of the following statements is/are true about enteric coated drugs?

Untitled Multiple Choice

false

The Cytochrome P450 enzymes that help metabolise drugs are found only in the liver

Untitled True/False Question

true

'Bioavailability' is the fraction of the administered dose that gets into systemic circulation unchanged. Intravenous administration has 100% bioavailability, while other modes of administration are variable. If the medication has a very low bioavailability by other means, it may need to be intravenously administered.

===== Enzymatic inhibition by drugs, Part 1 [_7758387_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758183_1?view=content&state=view
Learning about enzymatic inhibition
Why is it that irreversible enzymatic inhibitors like aspirin and NSAIDs affect platelets for the life of the platelet (9 days) but only decrease inflammation for four hours? How can our drugs affect enzymes? So much to learn about enzymes and how our medications affect them but learning about it is interesting.
Video: Enzymatic inhibition by drugs, Part 1
Get a really good picture of the second most important way in which our medications work by clicking onto the video.
Activity: Test your knowledge

--- h5p_d51379eebd12e24f5ab8 ---
Competitive inhibition is a type of reversible enzymatic inhibition in which the inhibitor is competing with the substrate. When the medication is fully metabolised, there should be no further effects

Non-competitive inhibition is a type of reversible enzymatic inhibition that involves the drug binding to the enzyme and changing the shape of the enzyme so that the natural substrate does not bind.

Irreversible enzymatic inhibition destroys the ability of the cell to produce that enzyme

--- h5p_8e23a332176d2f3b5153 ---
Omeprazole is enteric coated which makes the drug last longer in the system

Simplistically speaking, Omeprazole effectively stops the energy supply to the hydrogen potassium pump, while Famotidine is just a competitive antagonist, simply competing with Histamine 2, which is needed for the hydrogen-potassium pump to pump.

Omeprazole is an irreversible inhibitor of the Hydrogen potassium ATPase pump, and since that is such an elaborate protein/pump, it takes quite a while for the parietal cells to either degrade the drug in the parietal cell, or to produce more of that enzymatic pump.

The side effects of enzymatic inhibitors
How is it that something like grapefruit juice (and many of our drugs) can make some drugs more potent and other drugs less potent? How can drugs 'induce' some enzymes while inhibiting others? In this lesson, we continue using the proton pump inhibitor Omeprazole to learn about enzymes and how drugs can affect enzymes.
Video: Understanding enzymatic inhibitors
Enzymatic inhibition is the second most important way in which our medications work, so continue your understanding about enzymatic inhibition by engaging in this video lesson.

--- h5p_60416950de6e6b99a2a4 ---
Effect on Drug A: The enzyme which is inhibited may be responsible for converting a pro-drug to an active drug, and if that reaction is inhibited, the pro-drug version of Drug A would remain inactive

Effect on Drug B: The enzyme may be responsible for changing an active drug to an inactive drug, and if that reaction is inhibited, the active drug version of Drug B may remain active for longer than expected

Effect on Drug C: The particular enzyme affected is not involved in the metabolism of the drug, and that will result in respiratory distress, coma and death.

Finish ?

Retry ?

If a drug (or for that matter, a food like grapefruit) inadvertently affects one of the Cytochrome P450 enzymes that metabolises other drugs, which of the following may occur?

Untitled Multiple Choice

true

One of the main reasons that an enzymatic inhibitor has side effects is that the enzyme that needs to be affected to exert therapeutic effects may have other roles in the body which we do not want to affect. For instance, NSAIDs and aspirin inhibit the enzyme COX, which gives those medications the ability to reduce inflammation. But COX has protective roles throughout the body, and those are affected as one takes NSAIDs and affecting THOSE result in the side effects.

===== Omeprazole [_7758390_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758186_1?view=content&state=view
Video: Omeprazole
Omeprazole is a proton pump inhibitor that is nearly 100% effective in reducing stomach acid and as such is the first line therapy for peptic ulcer disease and GORD/GERD. Pregnancy categories are also briefly reiterated as Omeprazole is a pregnancy category B3 drug.

--- h5p_6002ab22d34286c66381 ---
Since proton pump inhibitors such as Omeprazole and other ...prazole drugs decrease stomach acid by nearly 100%, they are the first line therapy for people with Barrett's Disease and Peptic Ulcer Disease.

Barrett's disease is a condition where there is oesophageal dysplasia, and it may progress to cancer if there is continued insult involving acid spashing up on the cells.

Peptic ulcer disease can result in a clinical emergency because continued acid can erode into a blood vessel, resulting in bleeding into the stomach.

If a client has been on proton pump inhibitors like omeprazole for 5 or more years, the person should be withdrawn from the medication

Untitled Multiple Choice

Omeprazole is considered a pregnancy category B3 medication. Explain what that means, remembering that all three subsets of pregnancy category B involve this as the starting point: Medicines which have been taken by only a limited number of pregnant women and women of childbearing age, without an increase in the frequency of malformation or other direct or indirect harmful effects on the human foetus having been observed"
Since there are insufficient data on HUMANS, THE SECOND HALF OF THE DESCRIPTION HAS TO DO WITH WHAT WAS FOUND IN ANIMALS. BRIEFLY EXPLAIN THAT PORTION FOR A B3 medication

Untitled Essay

true

The second portion of the description of a pregnancy category B3 medication involves animal studies. In those studies, there was some harm to the foetus but we do not know if that is applicable to humans.

Untitled Multiple Choice

Intestinal obstruction

Appendicitis

Gallstones

Perforated bowel

anything that greatly affects the level of glucose, electrolytes, acid, base, or thyroid hormone

Psychological stress that is being suppressed

None of the other choices in this list are correct (Each of these choices can cause vomiting and should be considered before an antiemetic is given to the client)

Which of the following is NOT a cause of vomiting?

true

One of the problems with vomiting is that the VOMITING ITSELF CAN CAUSE fluid, acid/base, and electrolyte abnormalities that become a secondary problem that is a clinical emergency. Therefore, vomiting itself can cause neurological and/or cardiovascular abnormalities, coma and death.

===== Antiemetics for motion sickness [_7758190_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758190_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Antiemetics for motion sickness

===== Antiemetics for motion sickness [_7758394_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758190_1?view=content&state=view
Treating motion sickness
Since there are four completely different pathways to the vomiting centre, using four distinctly different sets of neurotransmitters, emesis (vomiting) needs to be treated with the correct antiemetic.
This lesson helps you understand which antiemetics must be used to address motion sickness and which ones should be used for the type of vomiting that originates from the higher brain centre (like the vomiting that occurs when you think "looking at that just made me sick"').
Video: Antiemetics for motion sickness
Condensed version of antiemetics
Activity: Test your knowledge

--- panopto_d20bfd7f-6513-4c94-a54f-7fe351f9b541 ---
[0.80] The vomiting centre in the medulla can be activated directly by irritants or indirectly following input from four major areas.
[14.24] The first area is the cerebral cortex,
[17.28] so an example of some stimuli that would prompt the cerebral cortex and send messages over to the vomiting centre would be extreme pain or anxiety.
[31.60] And examples of drugs that would decrease the inputs from the cerebral cortex include the benzodiazepines.
[41.68] Another area that can send messages over to the vomiting centre in the medulla is the vestibular area.
[51.24] An example of a condition that originates in the vestibular area and can prompt nausea and vomiting is of course,
[58.84] motion sickness, and the neurotransmitters that are involved in motion sickness are the histamine one and the acetylcholine.
[70.72] So the best medications for motion sickness are usually going to be the histamine one antagonists,
[79.24] or in other words, the antihistamines and the acetylcholine antagonists.
[87.48] The last two areas that can prompt nausea and vomiting include from the vagal nerve of the GI.
[95.96] For instance, if there is damage to the GI and from the chemo receptor trigger zone, which is up right next to the vomiting centre in the medulla.
[109.48] Both of those areas use serotonin as the neurotransmitter.
[114.92] And both of those areas are affected as a person goes through chemotherapy.
[121.11] In most instances, ondansetron and other drugs are the most effective drugs for relieving nausea and vomiting from chemotherapy.
[133.43] So just knowing the pathways you see, you can actually predict the effectiveness of antiemetic.
[142.35] One would not expect ondansetron to be an effective agent in decreasing emesis from motion sickness, for instance,
[152.15] because the pathways that go from the vestibular area into the vomiting centre are not going to be mediated by serotonin.
[163.07] In that instance, you would actually choose one of the first generation antihistamines, or the anticholinergic agents to decrease the motion sickness.

Untitled True/False Question

Pepto-Bismol
(Bismuth Subsalicylate) is considered an antiemetic

Bismuth subsalicylate has local anti-inflammatory effects

Bismuth subsalicylate decreases stomach acid

Bismuth subsalicylate has some local, direct antimicrobial effects.

Bismuth subsalicylate is not generally sold in Australia and as such, no questions will be in the quiz regarding that remedy, however this is important to know as clients from overseas will be asking where to find it.

===== Neurotransmitters [_7758193_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758193_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Neurotransmitters

===== Neurotransmitters [_7758397_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758193_1?view=content&state=view
What are neurotransmitters?
Neurons communicate with the adjacent neuron using a neurotransmitter that excites or inhibits the next neuron. But we need to get that neurotransmitter back out of the area very quickly by a number of methods, so that there is not constant excitation or inhibition at the next neuron. Our medications that affect neurotransmission can target the receptors on the neurons, they can mimic the neurotransmitter, or they can target the ways in which we get the neurotransmitters out of the synaptic cleft. The antiemetics all target the receptors on the neurons. In other words, they are receptor antagonists for the neurotransmitters involved in emesis.
Video: Understanding neurotransmission
We discuss neurotransmission in Module 5, but it would be good to know some of that information as we discuss antiemetic drugs right now.
Activity: Test your knowledge

https://learn.scu.edu.au/bbcswebdav/pid-7758397-dt-content-rid-88841116_2/xid-88841116_2

As a receptor antagonist

By blocking the reuptake of the neurotransmitter into the original cell.

By degrading the neurotransmitter in the synaptic cleft

By inhibiting the enzyme that degrades the neurotransmitter.

Finish ?

Retry ?

Medications that work on neurons can work by at least four different mechanisms of action. Which way do almost all antiemetics work?

Untitled Multiple Choice

true

There are four separate pathways that go to the vomiting centre, and they all use different neurotransmitters. This means that the correct medication needs to be chosen, or the antiemetic will have no therapeutic effects.

Untitled Multiple Choice

Intestinal obstruction

Appendicitis

Gallstones

Perforated bowel

anything that greatly affects the level of glucose, electrolytes, acid, base, or thyroid hormone

Psychological stress that is being suppressed

None of the other choices in this list are correct (Each of these choices can cause vomiting and should be considered before an antiemetic is given to the client)

Which of the following is NOT a cause of vomiting?

true

One of the problems with vomiting is that the VOMITING ITSELF CAN CAUSE fluid, acid/base, and electrolyte abnormalities that become a secondary problem that is a clinical emergency. Therefore, vomiting itself can cause neurological and/or cardiovascular abnormalities, coma and death.

===== Antiemetics for motion sickness [_7758195_1] =====

Antiemetics for motion sickness

===== Antiemetics for motion sickness [_7758399_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758195_1?view=content&state=view
Treating motion sickness
Since there are four completely different pathways to the vomiting centre, using four distinctly different sets of neurotransmitters, emesis (vomiting) needs to be treated with the correct antiemetic. This lesson helps you understand which antiemetics must be used to address motion sickness and which ones should be used for the type of vomiting that originates from the higher brain centre (like the vomiting that occurs when you think "looking at that just made me sick"').
Video: Antiemetics for motion sickness
Activity: Test your knowledge

Module summary
Congratulations on completing the most difficult topics in Pharmacology!
Please reward yourself for giving this module your best shot. You will now understand the material in subsequent chapters, and most of the discussions about medications that you will engage in with colleagues, and you will be able to explain medications to clients. You have also begun your preparation for the 30% end of Term quiz.
See you in Module 2!
Warm regards, Ken
